(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system,
\eta\in[\ell]^{\ell}
be an indexing tuple,
A\subset[\ell]
be a subset of indices and
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell},\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{\ell},q_{1},\ldots,q_{L}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
be polynomials. Suppose that

(i)

the tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
is a descendant of
\mathopen{}\left(T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
;

(ii)

f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
are 1-bounded functions having the
\gamma
-invariance property along
\eta
with respect to
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
.

Then there exist 1-bounded functions
f_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,f^{\prime}_{\ell}
, polynomials
\rho^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\rho^{\prime}_{\ell},q_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,q_{L}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
, and an indexing tuple
\eta^{\prime}
with the following properties:

(i)

the tuple
\eta^{\prime}
takes the form

\displaystyle\eta^{\prime}_{j}=\begin{cases}j,\;&j\in A\\
\eta_{j},\;&j\notin A;\end{cases}

(ii)

\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta^{\prime}_{j}}^{\rho^{\prime}_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
is a descendant of
\mathopen{}\left(T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
;

(iii)

f^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,f^{\prime}_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
are 1-bounded and have the
\gamma
-invariance property along
\eta^{\prime}
with respect to
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
; moreover, for every
s\geq 2
and
j\in[\ell]
, they satisfy the bound
\lvert\!|\!|f^{\prime}_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta^{\prime}_{j}}}\leq C\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{j}}}
for some
C>0
depending only on
\gamma
and the leading coefficients of
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
; lastly,
f^{\prime}_{j}=1
whenever
f_{j}=1
;

(iv)

we have the inequality

(76)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{{\eta_{j}}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{{\eta^{\prime}_{j}}}^{\rho^{\prime}_{j}(n)}f^{\prime}_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q^{\prime}_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

We note that if the leading coefficients of
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
are all 1, and the good invariance property takes the form of
f_{j}
being invariant under
T_{\eta_{j}}T_{j}^{-1}
for all
j\in[\ell]
, then Proposition
6.6
is straightforward, and in fact for every
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we have

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{{\eta_{j}}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}f_{j}\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))=\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta^{\prime}_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}f_{j}\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))

and
\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{j}}}=\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta^{\prime}_{j}}}
for all
j\in[\ell],s\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
The need for the more complicated statement of Proposition
6.6
comes from tedious but uninspiring technicalities that appear when the polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
have leading coefficients distinct from 1.

We have used the flipping technique twice in Example
7
: in (
57
) and when passing from (
60
) to (
61
) in order to obtain a seminorm control on the former using the seminorm control on the latter. We also used it in Example
8
to get seminorm control of (
71
). We will also use it shortly to handle uncontrollable tuples.

Proof of Proposition
6.6
.

For each
j\in[\ell]
, let
a_{j}
be the leading coefficient of
p_{j}
and
\gamma_{j}\in{\mathbb{N}}
be the smallest natural number such that
f_{j}
is invariant under
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{j}}^{a_{\eta_{j}}}T_{j}^{-a_{j}}\mathclose{}\right)^{\gamma_{j}}
(in particular,
\gamma_{j}=1
if
\eta_{j}=j
). Let
\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}
be the smallest natural number such that
a_{\eta_{j}}\gamma_{j}
divides the coefficients of
\gamma\rho_{j}
for every
j\in A
.
We then define

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{j}:=T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(r)}f_{j},\quad q^{\prime}_{j}(n):=q_{j}(\gamma n+r),\quad\rho^{\prime}_{j}(n):=\frac{a_{\eta^{\prime}_{j}}}{a_{\eta_{j}}}\mathopen{}\left(\rho_{j}(\gamma n+r)-\rho_{j}(r)\mathclose{}\right)

for some
r\in\{0,\ldots,\lambda-1\}
to be chosen later, and we observe that
\rho^{\prime}_{j}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
for every
j\in[\ell]
. By definition of
\eta^{\prime}
and the
\gamma
-invariance of
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}
along
\eta
, the functions
f_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,f_{\ell}^{\prime}
are
\gamma
-invariant along
\eta^{\prime}
. Moreover, if
f_{j}=1
, then so is
f^{\prime}_{j}
. Lastly, they satisfy the bound
\lvert\!|\!|f^{\prime}_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta^{\prime}_{j}}}\ll_{a_{\eta_{j}}\gamma}\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{j}}}
for every
s\geq 2
and
j\in[\ell]
; this is trivial for
j\notin A
, and if
j\in A
, then

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f^{\prime}_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta^{\prime}_{j}}}=\lvert\!|\!|f^{\prime}_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{j}}=\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{j}}\leq\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{j}^{a_{j}\gamma}}=\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{j}}^{a_{\eta_{j}}\gamma}}\ll_{a_{\eta_{j}}\gamma}\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{j}}},

where we use the fact that
f_{j}
is a composition of
f^{\prime}_{j}
with respect to a measure preserving transformation, the invariance property of
f_{j}
, Lemma
3.5
, and both directions of Lemma
2.1
.

We move on to prove the inequality (
76
), For
j\notin A
, where
\eta^{\prime}_{j}=\eta_{j}
, we simply have
T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(\gamma n+r)}f_{j}=T_{\eta^{\prime}_{j}}^{\rho^{\prime}_{j}(n)}f_{j}^{\prime}
. For
j\in A
, the invariance property of
f_{j}
gives us the identity

\displaystyle T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(\gamma n+r)}f_{j}=\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{j}}^{a_{\eta_{j}}\gamma_{j}}\mathclose{}\right)^{\frac{\rho_{j}(\gamma n+r)-\rho_{j}(r)}{a_{\eta_{j}}\gamma_{j}}}T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(r)}f_{j}=T_{\eta^{\prime}_{j}}^{\rho^{\prime}_{j}(n)}f_{j}^{\prime}.

Splitting
{\mathbb{N}}
into
(\gamma\cdot{\mathbb{N}}+r)_{r\in\{0,\ldots,\gamma-1\}}
, we deduce from the pigeonhole principle that there exists
r\in\{0,\ldots,\gamma-1\}
for which (
76
) holds.

From the construction of the polynomials
\rho_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\rho^{\prime}_{\ell}
, the assumption that
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
is a descendant of
\mathopen{}\left(T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
and Lemma
6.2
, it follows that
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta^{\prime}_{j}}^{\rho^{\prime}_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
is also a descendant of
\mathopen{}\left(T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
.
∎

6.3.
Handling uncontrollable tuples

We have explained in the previous sections that if a tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
is controllable, then we control it by a Gowers-Host-Kra seminorm using a seminorm smoothing argument. If the tuple is uncontrollable, however, we use the following variant of the flipping technique from Proposition
6.6
to bound the
L^{2}(\mu)
norm of the associated average by an
L^{2}(\mu)
norm of a controllable average.

Corollary 6.7

(Flipping uncontrollable tuples)
.

Let
\gamma,\ell,L\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system,
\eta\in[\ell]^{\ell}
be an indexing tuple, and
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell},\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{\ell},q_{1},\ldots,q_{L}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
be polynomials. Suppose that

(i)

the tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
is uncontrollable of type
w
with the last nonzero index
t_{w}
, and it is a descendant of
\mathopen{}\left(T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
;

(ii)

f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
are 1-bounded functions having the
\gamma
-invariance property along
\eta
with respect to
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
.

Then there exist 1-bounded functions
f_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,f^{\prime}_{\ell}
, polynomials
\rho^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\rho^{\prime}_{\ell},q_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,q_{L}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
, and an indexing tuple
\eta^{\prime}
with the following properties:

(i)

the tuple
\eta^{\prime}
takes the form

\displaystyle\eta^{\prime}_{j}=\begin{cases}j,\;&\eta_{j}\in{\mathfrak{I}}_{t_{w}}\\
\eta_{j},\;&\eta_{j}\notin{\mathfrak{I}}_{t_{w}};\end{cases}

(ii)

\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta^{\prime}_{j}}^{\rho^{\prime}_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
is a descendant of
\mathopen{}\left(T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
;

(iii)

f^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,f^{\prime}_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
are 1-bounded and have the
\gamma
-invariance property along
\eta^{\prime}
with respect to
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
; moreover, for every
s\geq 2
and
j\in[\ell]
, they satisfy the bound
\lvert\!|\!|f^{\prime}_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta^{\prime}_{j}}}\leq C\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{j}}}
for some
C>0
depending only on
\gamma
and the leading coefficients of
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
; lastly,
f^{\prime}_{j}=1
whenever
f_{j}=1
;

(iv)

we have the inequality

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{{\eta_{j}}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{{\eta^{\prime}_{j}}}^{\rho^{\prime}_{j}(n)}f^{\prime}_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q^{\prime}_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Corollary
6.7
follows from Proposition
6.6
by taking
A=\{j\in[\ell]:\eta_{j}\in{\mathfrak{I}}_{t_{w}}\}
.

We emphasise that Corollary
6.7
by itself does not guarantee that the tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta^{\prime}_{j}}^{\rho^{\prime}_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
has a lower type than the tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
. However, this will be the case when we apply it to all the tuples
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
that appear in our inductive procedure. The crucial ingredient in achieving this type reduction will be the property (iv) in Proposition
6.8
enjoyed by all the tuples showing up in our arguments.

Example 9
.

Consider the tuple

(77)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(T_{1}^{n^{2}},T_{2}^{n^{2}},T_{3}^{n^{2}},T_{4}^{n^{2}},T_{1}^{n^{2}+n},T_{1}^{n^{2}+n},T_{5}^{n^{2}+2n},T_{5}^{n^{2}+2n}\mathclose{}\right)

from Example
4
. It is a descendant of the tuple

(78)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(T_{1}^{n^{2}},T_{2}^{n^{2}},T_{3}^{n^{2}},T_{4}^{n^{2}},T_{5}^{n^{2}+n},T_{6}^{n^{2}+n},T_{7}^{n^{2}+2n},T_{8}^{n^{2}+2n}\mathclose{}\right),

obtained by four applications of Proposition
6.1
, in which we substitute
T_{5}
for
T_{8}
at the index 8,
T_{5}
for
T_{7}
at the index 7,
T_{1}
for
T_{5}
at the index 5 and
T_{1}
for
T_{6}
at the index 6. Corollary
6.7
gives that if
f_{1},\ldots,f_{8}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
are functions such that
f_{5},f_{6},f_{7},f_{8}
are invariant under
T_{5}T_{1}^{-1},T_{6}T_{1}^{-1},T_{7}T_{5}^{-1},T_{8}T_{5}^{-1}
respectively, then we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n^{2}}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}}f_{2}\cdot T_{3}^{n^{2}}f_{3}\cdot T_{4}^{n^{2}}f_{4}\cdot T_{1}^{n^{2}+n}f_{5}\cdot T_{1}^{n^{2}+n}f_{6}\cdot T_{5}^{n^{2}+2n}f_{7}\cdot T_{5}^{n^{2}+2n}f_{8}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}T_{1}^{n^{2}}f_{1}\cdot T_{2}^{n^{2}}f_{2}\cdot T_{3}^{n^{2}}f_{3}\cdot T_{4}^{n^{2}}f_{4}\cdot T_{1}^{n^{2}+n}f_{5}\cdot T_{1}^{n^{2}+n}f_{6}\cdot T_{7}^{n^{2}+2n}f_{7}\cdot T_{8}^{n^{2}+2n}f_{8}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

(in fact, a closer look guarantees that we get an equality and not just for the
L^{2}(\mu)
limits, but for each finite average). We moreover obtain the equality of seminorms
\lvert\!|\!|f_{7}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{7}}=\lvert\!|\!|f_{7}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{5}},\lvert\!|\!|f_{8}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{8}}=\lvert\!|\!|f_{8}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{5}},

and the new tuple

(79)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(T_{1}^{n^{2}},T_{2}^{n^{2}},T_{3}^{n^{2}},T_{4}^{n^{2}},T_{1}^{n^{2}+n},T_{1}^{n^{2}+n},T_{7}^{n^{2}+2n},T_{8}^{n^{2}+2n}\mathclose{}\right)

is a descendant of (
78
). Importantly, the new tuple (
79
) has type
(6,0,2)
, which is lower than the type
(6,2,0)
of (
77
). Applying Corollary
6.7
to (
77
), we have thus successfully replaced it by a tuple of lower type. Lastly, the new tuple (
79
) is controllable as the indices
7,8
satisfy the controllability condition.

6.4.
Recapitulation

We conclude this section with Proposition
6.8
, which forms an inductive framework for the proof of Theorem
1.1
in the next section. Combining the content of Proposition
6.1
and Corollary
6.7
, it shows that - starting with a tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
satisfying the good ergodicity property - we reach a tuple of basic type in a finite number of steps. For tuples of basic type, seminorm control will follow from arguments made in Proposition
7.2
, and then we will use this fact and induction to go back and get seminorm control for the original tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
.

Proposition 6.8

(Inductive framework)
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
be polynomials, and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system. Suppose that the tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
has the good ergodicity property. Then there exists
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
(which depends only on
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
but can be bounded purely in terms of
\ell
)
and a sequence of tuples

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{0j}}^{\rho_{0j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}\to\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{1j}}^{\rho_{1j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}\to\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{2j}}^{\rho_{2j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}\to\ldots\to\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{rj}}^{\rho_{rj}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}

with types
w_{0},\ldots,w_{r}
such that
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{0j}}^{\rho_{0j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}:=\mathopen{}\left(T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
and for
k\in\{0,\ldots,r-1\}
, the following properties hold.

(i)

If the tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{kj}}^{\rho_{kj}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
is controllable, then the tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{(k+1)j}}^{\rho_{(k+1)j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
is chosen using Proposition
6.1
.

(ii)

If the tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{kj}}^{\rho_{kj}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
is uncontrollable, then the tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{(k+1)j}}^{\rho_{(k+1)j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
is chosen using Corollary
6.7
.

(iii)

The tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{(k+1)j}}^{\rho_{(k+1)j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
is a descendant of
\mathopen{}\left(T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
. In particular, it has the good ergodicity property.

(iv)

For the indexing tuple
\eta_{k+1}=(\eta_{(k+1)1},\ldots,\eta_{(k+1)\ell})
, if
j\in{\mathfrak{I}}_{t}
, then either
\eta_{(k+1)j}=j
or
\eta_{(k+1)j}\in{\mathfrak{I}}_{t^{\prime}}
for some
t^{\prime}<t
.

(v)

We have
w_{k+1}<w_{k}
, i.e. the tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{(k+1)j}}^{\rho_{(k+1)j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
has a lower type than the tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{kj}}^{\rho_{kj}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
.

(vi)

The tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{rj}}^{\rho_{rj}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
has basic type and the restriction
\eta_{r}|_{{\mathfrak{I}}_{1}}
is the identity sequence.

For the rest of the paper, we call a tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
a
proper descendant
of the tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
if it appears in one of the sequences of tuples constructed from
\mathopen{}\left(T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
using Proposition
6.8
.

Proof.

Let
k\in\{0,\ldots,r-1\}
. Suppose that the tuples
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{0j}}^{\rho_{0j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]},\ldots,\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{kj}}^{\rho_{kj}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
are already constructed and satisfy the properties (i)-(v). If the tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{kj}}^{\rho_{kj}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
has basic type, we halt. Otherwise, we choose the tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{(k+1)j}}^{\rho_{(k+1)j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
using Proposition
6.1
if
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{kj}}^{\rho_{kj}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
is controllable
12
12

12

We remark that by the property (iv) applied to the tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{kj}}^{\rho_{kj}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
, we have
\eta_{kj}=j
whenever
j\in{\mathfrak{I}}_{1}
, hence the type
w_{k}=(w_{k1},\ldots,w_{k\ell})
of
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{kj}}^{\rho_{kj}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
satisfies
w_{k1}
¿0. By the assumption that
w_{k}
is not basic, we also have
w_{kt}>0
for some
t>1
, therefore we have at least two nonzero indices in
w_{k}
and we can act as in Proposition
6.1
.
and using Corollary
6.7
otherwise, so that the properties (i) and (ii) are satisfied. We note from Corollaries
6.3
and
6.7
that the new tuple is a descendant of
\mathopen{}\left(T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
, and by Proposition
6.4
it has the good ergodicity property, hence the property (iii) holds as well. The property (iv) holds by induction and the way the tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{(k+1)j}}^{\rho_{(k+1)j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
is constructed using Proposition
6.1
or Corollary
6.7
.

For the property (v), we first note that if
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{kj}}^{\rho_{kj}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
is controllable, then the property (v) holds for
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{(k+1)j}}^{\rho_{(k+1)j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
by Proposition
6.1
. If
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{kj}}^{\rho_{kj}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
is uncontrollable, then we get from Corollary
6.7
that
w_{(k+1)t_{k}}=0<w_{kt_{k}}
, where
t_{k}:=t_{w_{k}}
is the last nonzero index of
w_{k}
, and we deduce from property (iv) that
w_{(k+1)t}=w_{kt}
for
1\leq t<t_{k}
. This point is important, so we explain it in words. What happens is that when we apply Corollary
6.7
, the index
w_{(k+1)t_{k}}
goes down to 0 (since all the transformations with indices from
{\mathfrak{I}}_{t_{k}}
get flipped), but the new transformations appearing in their place have indices from
{\mathfrak{I}}_{t_{k}+1},\ldots,{\mathfrak{I}}_{K_{2}}
, as given by the property (iv). Hence,
w_{k+1}<w_{k}
in this case as well.

It follows from the property (v) and the fact that there are at most
(K_{3}+1)^{K_{2}}
possible types for tuples in
{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{K_{2}}
with sum of coordinates
K_{3}
, that the sequence eventually terminates. And since, by our construction, it can only terminate on the basic type

(K_{3},0,\ldots,0)
,
there exists
r<(K_{3}+1)^{K_{2}}\leq(\ell+1)^{\ell}
such that the tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{rj}}^{\rho_{rj}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
has basic type
(K_{3},0,\ldots,0)
. The second part of property (vi) for this tuple follows from the property (iv) by taking
t=1
.
∎

Example 10

(Iterative reduction to tuples of lower type)
.

For the tuple (
78
) from Example
9
, Proposition
6.8
would give, among other options, the following sequence of tuples:

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(T_{1}^{n^{2}},T_{2}^{n^{2}},T_{3}^{n^{2}},T_{4}^{n^{2}},T_{5}^{n^{2}+n},T_{6}^{n^{2}+n},T_{7}^{n^{2}+2n},T_{8}^{n^{2}+2n}\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(T_{1}^{n^{2}},T_{2}^{n^{2}},T_{3}^{n^{2}},T_{4}^{n^{2}},T_{5}^{n^{2}+n},T_{6}^{n^{2}+n},T_{7}^{n^{2}+2n},T_{5}^{n^{2}+2n}\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(T_{1}^{n^{2}},T_{2}^{n^{2}},T_{3}^{n^{2}},T_{4}^{n^{2}},T_{5}^{n^{2}+n},T_{6}^{n^{2}+n},T_{5}^{n^{2}+2n},T_{5}^{n^{2}+2n}\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(T_{1}^{n^{2}},T_{2}^{n^{2}},T_{3}^{n^{2}},T_{4}^{n^{2}},T_{1}^{n^{2}+n},T_{6}^{n^{2}+n},T_{5}^{n^{2}+2n},T_{5}^{n^{2}+2n}\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(T_{1}^{n^{2}},T_{2}^{n^{2}},T_{3}^{n^{2}},T_{4}^{n^{2}},T_{1}^{n^{2}+n},T_{1}^{n^{2}+n},T_{5}^{n^{2}+2n},T_{5}^{n^{2}+2n}\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(T_{1}^{n^{2}},T_{2}^{n^{2}},T_{3}^{n^{2}},T_{4}^{n^{2}},T_{1}^{n^{2}+n},T_{1}^{n^{2}+n},T_{7}^{n^{2}+2n},T_{8}^{n^{2}+2n}\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(T_{1}^{n^{2}},T_{2}^{n^{2}},T_{3}^{n^{2}},T_{4}^{n^{2}},T_{1}^{n^{2}+n},T_{1}^{n^{2}+n},T_{7}^{n^{2}+2n},T_{2}^{n^{2}+2n}\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(T_{1}^{n^{2}},T_{2}^{n^{2}},T_{3}^{n^{2}},T_{4}^{n^{2}},T_{1}^{n^{2}+n},T_{1}^{n^{2}+n},T_{2}^{n^{2}+2n},T_{2}^{n^{2}+2n}\mathclose{}\right).

Their types, starting from the top tuple, are

\displaystyle(4,2,2)>(4,3,1)>(4,4,0)>(5,3,0)>(6,2,0)>(6,0,2)>(7,0,1)>(8,0,0),

which shows that at each step, the new tuple has a lower type than its predecessor. The sixth tuple, counting from the top, has been obtained via Corollary
6.7
(since the fifth tuple is uncontrollable, as explained in Examples
4
and
9
) while all the other tuples have been obtained using Proposition
6.1
. Each subsequent tuple is a descendant of the original tuple, and so each of them has the good ergodicity property thanks to Proposition
6.4
. Lastly, the final tuple has basic type, and moreover for its indexing tuple
\eta
, the restriction
\eta|_{{\mathfrak{I}}_{1}}=\eta|_{[4]}
is an identity because no substitution has taken place at the first four indices.

7.
The proof of Theorem
1.1

7.1.
Induction scheme

In all statements in this section, we work in the setting of Proposition
6.8
, i.e. all the lower type tuples at which we arrive from some original average are those constructed in Proposition
6.8
and have the properties listed there.

Theorem
1.1
follows by induction from the result below upon setting
d=L=0
and letting
\eta
be the identity tuple.

Proposition 7.1

(Seminorm control restated)
.

Let
d,D,\ell,L\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\eta\in[\ell]^{\ell}
and
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
,
q_{1},\ldots,q_{L}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
be polynomials of degrees at most D. Suppose that the polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
have the good ergodicity property for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
. Let
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
be a proper descendant of the tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
.
Then there exists
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
, depending only on
d,D,\ell,L
,
such that for all functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
with the good invariance property along
\eta
with respect to
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
and all sequences of functions
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{L}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}
, we have

(80)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{{\eta_{j}}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{j}}}=0
for some
j\in[\ell]
.

A word of explanation is necessary for the statement of Proposition
7.1
. We need both polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
and
\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{\ell}
. The reason is that for our induction to work, we need the functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}
to have the good invariance property with respect to the original family
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
rather than the descendant family
\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{\ell}
. This is necessary for a number of reasons: to prove seminorm control for averages of basic types in the proof of Proposition
7.2
; to apply Proposition
6.6
in the
pong
step of Proposition
7.5
; to derive Proposition
7.1
from Proposition
7.4
for controllable tuples; and to invoke Corollary
6.7
for uncontrollable tuples in Proposition
7.1
.
The necessity of keeping track of the invariance property with regards to the original polynomial family has also been explained in Step 2 of Example
8
.

Technically, the value
s
in Proposition
7.1
depends also on on
\eta
, but since the number of possible tuples
\eta
is bounded in terms of
\ell
, this dependence can be removed.

We first prove Proposition
7.1
for averages (
35
) of basic type. This will serve as the base for induction for Propositions
7.1
,
7.4
, and
7.5
.

Proposition 7.2

(Seminorm control of basic types)
.

Let
d,D,\ell,L\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\eta\in[\ell]^{\ell}
and
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
,
q_{1},\ldots,q_{L}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
be polynomials of degrees at most D. Suppose that the polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
have the good ergodicity property for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
. Let
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
be a proper descendant of the tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
, and suppose that the type
w
of
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
is basic.
Then there exists
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
, depending only on
d,D,\ell,L
,
such that for all 1-bounded functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
with the good invariance property along
\eta
with respect to
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
and all sequences of functions
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{L}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}
, we have (
80
) whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{j}}}=0
for some
j\in[\ell]
.

A special case of Proposition
7.2
has been sketched in Step 2 of Example
8
, and we invite the reader to compare the abstract proof presented below with the argument in Step 2 of Example
8
.

Proof.

We induct on the length
\ell
of the average. If
\ell=1
, the statement holds by Proposition
3.7
. We therefore assume that
\ell>1
, and we will prove Proposition
7.2
for fixed
\ell>1
by invoking Proposition
7.1
for an average of length
\ell-1
. More specifically, we will show first that there exists an index
m
satisfying the controllability condition, and that
we can control the average by a
T_{\eta_{m}}
-seminorm of
f_{m}
. Then we will replace
f_{m}
by a dual function using Proposition
2.3
and the pigeonhole principle, flip the other transformations
T_{\eta_{j}}
into
T_{j}
using Proposition
6.6
, and invoke Proposition
7.1
for averages of length
\ell-1
to obtain seminorm control in terms of other functions.

Take any
m\in{\mathfrak{I}}_{1}
; we assume for simplicity that
m=\ell
. Proposition
6.8
(vi) implies that
\eta_{\ell}=\ell
, and moreover that
\ell
satisfies the controllability condition. This fact and Proposition
3.7
imply that the identity (
80
) holds whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s}}=0
for some

(81)

\displaystyle{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s}\in\{b_{\ell}{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell}-b_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{i}}:\ i\in{\mathfrak{L}}\cup\{0\}\setminus\{\ell\}\},

where
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
depends only on
d,D,\ell,L
, the numbers
b_{\ell},b_{i}
are the coefficients of
\rho_{\ell},\rho_{i}
of degree
d_{\ell i}:=\deg(\rho_{\ell}{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell}-\rho_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{i}})
whenever
i\neq 0
, and for
i=0
, we simply set
b_{0}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{0}}=\mathbf{0}
. Using the monotonicity property (
17
), we can assume without loss of generality that
s\geq 2
.
Since the tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
has a basic type, it follows that the indices
\eta_{i}
in (
81
) come from the set
{\mathfrak{I}}_{1}
. We have to show that each transformation
T^{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,T^{{\textbf{b}}_{s}}
is either a nonzero iterate of
T_{\ell}
or its invariant functions are invariant under a bounded power of
T_{\ell}
. For
k\in[s]
, let
{\textbf{b}}_{k}:=b_{\ell}{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell}-b_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{i}}
. If
i=0
, then
T^{{\textbf{b}}_{k}}
is indeed a nonzero iterate of
T_{\ell}
. If
i\in{\mathfrak{I}}_{1}
, then
\eta_{i}=i\neq\ell
by the property from Proposition
6.8
(vi) that
\eta|_{{\mathfrak{I}}_{1}}
is the identity tuple.
Then
T^{{\textbf{b}}_{k}}=T_{\ell}^{b_{\ell}}T_{i}^{-b_{i}}=\mathopen{}\left(T_{\ell}^{\beta_{\ell}}T_{i}^{-\beta_{i}}\mathclose{}\right)^{\gcd(b_{\ell},b_{i})}
for coprime integers
\beta_{\ell},\beta_{i}
, and the good ergodicity property of
\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{\ell}
along
\eta
(Propositions
6.8
(iii)) implies that
{\mathcal{I}}(T_{\ell}^{\beta_{\ell}}T_{i}^{-\beta_{i}})\subseteq{\mathcal{I}}(T_{\ell})
.
If
i\notin{\mathfrak{I}}_{1}\cup\{0\}
, then we split into the cases
\eta_{i}\neq\ell
and
\eta_{i}=\ell
. In the former case, we once again use the good ergodicity property of
\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{\ell}
along
\eta
to conclude that
T^{{\textbf{b}}_{k}}=\mathopen{}\left(T_{\ell}^{\beta_{\ell}}T_{i}^{-\beta_{i}}\mathclose{}\right)^{\gcd(b_{\ell},b_{i})}
and
{\mathcal{I}}(T_{\ell}^{\beta_{\ell}}T_{i}^{-\beta_{i}})\subseteq{\mathcal{I}}(T_{\ell})
.
In the latter case, the pairwise independence of
\rho_{i}
and
\rho_{\ell}
implies that
{\textbf{b}}_{k}
is nonzero, and hence
T^{{\textbf{b}}_{k}}
is a nonzero iterate of
T_{\ell}
. Rearranging
{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s}}=\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{c_{1}{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell},\ldots,c_{s^{\prime}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell},{\textbf{b}}_{s^{\prime}+1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s}}

for some
0\leq s^{\prime}\leq s
, nonzero integers
c_{1},\ldots,c_{s^{\prime}}
and transformations
T^{{\textbf{b}}_{s^{\prime}+1}},\ldots,T^{{\textbf{b}}_{s}}
with the property that for every
j\in\{s^{\prime}+1,\ldots,s\}
, there exists
{\textbf{b}}^{\prime}_{j}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\ell}
and nonzero
c_{j}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
{\textbf{b}}_{j}=c_{j}{\textbf{b}}^{\prime}_{j}
and
{\mathcal{I}}(T^{{\textbf{b}}^{\prime}_{j}})\subseteq{\mathcal{I}}(T^{{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell}})
. By Lemmas
2.1
and
2.2
, we have

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s}}=\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{c_{1}{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell},\ldots,c_{s^{\prime}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell},c_{s^{\prime}+1}{\textbf{b}}^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}+1},\ldots,c_{s}{\textbf{b}}^{\prime}_{s}}\ll_{c_{1},\ldots,c_{s}}\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\ell}},

implying that (
80
) holds whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\ell}}=0
.

We now use the seminorm control at
\ell
with Proposition
2.3
and the pigeonhole principle to deduce that if (
80
) fails, then it also fails when
T_{\eta_{\ell}}^{\rho_{\ell}(n)}f_{\ell}
is replaced by a sequence
{\mathcal{D}}_{L+1}(\rho_{\ell}(n))
with
{\mathcal{D}}_{L+1}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{s}
. Letting
q_{L+1}:=\rho_{\ell}
for simplicity, it is enough to show that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell-1]}T_{{\eta_{j}}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L+1]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0

implies
\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s^{\prime},T_{\eta_{j}}}>0
for every
j\in[\ell-1]
and some
s^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
depending only on
d,D,\ell,L
. By Proposition
6.6
, there exist 1-bounded functions
f_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,f_{\ell-1}^{\prime}
and polynomials
\rho_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\rho_{\ell-1}^{\prime}
with the good ergodicity property for the system such that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell-1]}T_{{j}}^{\rho^{\prime}_{j}(n)}f^{\prime}_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L+1]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0

and
\lvert\!|\!|f^{\prime}_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s^{\prime},T_{j}}\leq C\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s^{\prime},T_{\eta_{j}}}
for all
j\in[\ell-1]
,
s^{\prime}\geq 1
and some constant
C>0
depending only on
\gamma
and the leading coefficients of
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
. Invoking inductively the case
\ell-1
of Proposition
7.1
, we deduce that
\lvert\!|\!|f^{\prime}_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s^{\prime},T_{j}}>0
for some
s^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
depending only on
d,D,\ell,L
, and hence
\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s^{\prime},T_{\eta_{j}}}>0
. This proves the claim.
∎

We also need the following quantitative version of Proposition
7.1
.

Proposition 7.3

(Soft quantitative estimates)
.

Let
d,D,\gamma,\ell,L\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\eta\in[\ell]^{\ell}
and
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
,
q_{1},\ldots,q_{L}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
be polynomials of degrees at most D. Suppose that the polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
have the good ergodicity property for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
. Let
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
be a proper descendant of the tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
.
Then there exists
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
, depending only on
d,D,\ell,L
,
with the following property: for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
, such that for all
1
-bounded functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
that are
\gamma
-invariant along
\eta
with respect to
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
and all sequences of functions
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{L}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{{\eta_{j}}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}<\varepsilon

whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{j}}}<\delta
for some
j\in[\ell]
.

Proof.

We prove Proposition
7.3
for fixed
d,D,\gamma,\ell,L,\eta
by assuming Proposition
7.1
for the same parameters.

Let
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
be as in the statement of Proposition
7.1
(so in particular it only depends on
d,D,\ell,L
) and
b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell}
be the leading coefficients of
\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{\ell}
. Fix
\pi\in[\ell]^{L}
. We first prove the following qualitative claim: for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell},g_{1},\ldots,g_{L}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, where
f_{j}
is
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{j}}^{b_{\eta_{j}}}T_{j}^{-b_{j}}\mathclose{}\right)^{\gamma}
-invariant for each
j\in[\ell]
and
g_{j}
is
{\mathcal{Z}}_{d}(T_{\pi_{j}})
-measurable for each
j\in[L]
, we have

(82)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}T^{q_{j}(n)}_{\pi_{j}}g_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{j}}}=0
for some
j\in[\ell]
.

Fix
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell},g_{1},\ldots,g_{L}
and suppose that (
82
) fails. Using Proposition
2.3
and the pigeonhole principle, we deduce that there exist dual functions
g_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,g_{L}^{\prime}
of
T_{\pi_{1}},\ldots,T_{\pi_{L}}
of level
d
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}T^{q_{j}(n)}_{\pi_{j}}g_{j}^{\prime}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0.

Setting
{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(n):=T_{\pi_{j}}^{n}g^{\prime}_{j}
and using Proposition
7.1
, we deduce that
\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{j}}}>0
for all
j\in[\ell]
, and so the claim follows.

We combine the claim above with Proposition
3.6
for

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}:=\begin{cases}{\mathcal{I}}\mathopen{}\left(\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{j}}^{b_{\eta_{j}}}T_{j}^{-b_{j}}\mathclose{}\right)^{\gamma}\mathclose{}\right),\;&1\leq j\leq\ell\\
{\mathcal{Z}}_{d}(T_{\pi_{j}}),\;&\ell+1\leq j\leq L,\end{cases}

deducing the following: for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta_{\pi}>0
such that for all 1-bounded functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell},g_{1},\ldots,g_{L}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
with
f_{j}\in L^{\infty}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{I}}\mathopen{}\left(\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{j}}^{b_{\eta_{j}}}T_{j}^{-b_{j}}\mathclose{}\right)^{\gamma}\mathclose{}\right)\mathclose{}\right)
for
j\in[\ell]
and
g_{j}\in L^{\infty}({\mathcal{Z}}_{d}(T_{\pi_{j}}))
for
j\in[L]
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}T^{q_{j}(n)}_{\pi_{j}}g_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}<\varepsilon

whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{j}}}<\delta_{\pi}
for some
j\in[\ell]
.

Proposition
7.3
follows by taking

\delta:=\min(\delta_{\pi}\colon\,\pi\in[\ell]^{L})
and recalling that dual functions of
T_{j}
of order
d
are
{\mathcal{Z}}_{d}(T_{j})
-measurable, hence for every
j\in[L]
, the sequence
{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))
has the form
{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))=T_{\pi_{j}}^{q_{j}(n)}g_{j}
for some
\pi_{j}\in[\ell]
and a 1-bounded
{\mathcal{Z}}_{d}(T_{j})
-measurable function
g_{j}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
.
∎

For controllable tuples, Proposition
7.1
will be deduced from the following result.

Proposition 7.4

(Iterated box seminorm smoothing)
.

Let
d,D,\ell,L\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\eta\in[\ell]^{\ell}
and
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
,
q_{1},\ldots,q_{L}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
be polynomials of degrees at most D. Suppose that the polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
have the good ergodicity property for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
. Let
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
be a proper descendant of the tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
. Suppose that
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
of a non-basic type is controllable, and let
m
be an index satisfying the controllability condition. Then there exist
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
, depending only on
d,D,\ell,L
, such that for all functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
with the good invariance property along
\eta
with respect to
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
and all sequences of functions
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{L}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}
, we obtain (
80
) whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{m}}}=0
.

Proposition
7.4
is a consequence of Proposition
3.7
, followed by an iterated application of the smoothing result given below.

Proposition 7.5

(Box seminorm smoothing)
.

Let
d,D,\ell,L\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\eta\in[\ell]^{\ell}
and
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
,
q_{1},\ldots,q_{L}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
be polynomials of degrees at most D. Suppose that the polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
have the good ergodicity property for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
. Let
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
be a proper descendant of the tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
.
Suppose that
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
of a non-basic type is controllable, and let
m
be an index satisfying the controllability condition.
Then for all
s\geq 2
and vectors
{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}
satisfying (
23
) there exists
s^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, depending only on
d,D,\ell,L,s
, with the following property: for all functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
with the good invariance property along
\eta
with respect to
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
and all sequences of functions
{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{D}}_{L}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}
, if
\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}}=0
implies (
80
), then (
80
) also holds under the assumption that
\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{m}}^{\times s^{\prime}}}=0
.

We explain now the induction scheme whereby we prove Propositions
7.1
-
7.5
. Roughly speaking, the proofs proceed by the induction on the length
\ell
of an average and - for fixed
\ell
- by induction on type, where the base case are averages of basic types. More precisely, the induction scheme goes as follows:

(i)

For tuples
(T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)})_{j\in[\ell]}
of length
\ell
of type
w
, Proposition
7.3
follows from Proposition
7.1
as proved before.

(ii)

Tuples
(T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)})_{j\in[\ell]}
of length
\ell=1
have necessarily basic type, and Propositions
7.1
and
7.2
follow easily from Proposition
3.7
.

(iii)

For tuples
(T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)})_{j\in[\ell]}
of length
\ell>1
and basic type, Proposition
7.1
is a consequence of Proposition
7.2
.

(iv)

For tuples
(T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)})_{j\in[\ell]}
of length
\ell>1
and non-basic type
w
, we prove Proposition
7.5
only under the assumption of controllability. This proof goes by inductively invoking Proposition
7.3
in two cases: for tuples of length
\ell
and type
w^{\prime}<w
, and for tuples of length
\ell-1
. An iterative application of Proposition
7.5
then yields Proposition
7.4
for tuples of length
\ell
and type
w
.

(v)

For controllable tuples
(T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)})_{j\in[\ell]}
of length
\ell>1
and non-basic type
w
, we prove Proposition
7.1
by invoking Proposition
7.4
for tuples of length
\ell
and type
w
followed by an application of Proposition
7.1
for tuples of length
\ell-1
.

(vi)

Lastly, for uncontrollable tuples
(T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)})_{j\in[\ell]}
of length
\ell>1
and non-basic type
w
, we prove Proposition
7.1
by inductively invoking Proposition
7.1
for tuples of length
\ell
and type
w^{\prime}<w
.

The way in which step (vi) is carried out has been illustrated in Example
9
, in which seminorm control for the uncontrollable average (
77
) is deduced from seminorm control for the lower-type controllable average (
79
). The example below summarises how steps (iv)-(v) proceed for a controllable average.

Example 11

(Inductive steps for a controllable average)
.

Consider the tuple

(83)

\displaystyle(T_{1}^{n^{2}},T_{2}^{n^{2}},T_{3}^{n^{2}+n},T_{2}^{n^{2}+n})

of type
(3,1)
,
which is a descendant of the tuple

(84)

\displaystyle(T_{1}^{n^{2}},T_{2}^{n^{2}},T_{3}^{n^{2}+n},T_{4}^{n^{2}+n})

from Example
7
of type
(2,2)
. While proving Proposition
7.5
for (
83
), we invoke in the
ping
step Proposition
7.3
for tuples

\displaystyle(T_{1}^{n^{2}},T_{2}^{n^{2}},T_{1}^{n^{2}+n},T_{2}^{n^{2}+n})\quad\textrm{and}\quad(T_{1}^{n^{2}},T_{2}^{n^{2}},T_{2}^{n^{2}+n},T_{2}^{n^{2}+n}).

They are proper descendants of the original tuple (
84
), have basic type
(4,0)
, and Proposition
7.1
follows for them from Proposition
7.2
. In the
pong
step of the proof of Proposition
7.5
for (
83
), we inductively invoke Proposition
7.3
for the following tuples of length 3, obtained by replacing the first and second term respectively by dual functions:

\displaystyle(*,T_{2}^{n^{2}},T_{3}^{n^{2}+n},T_{2}^{n^{2}+n})\quad\textrm{and}\quad(T_{1}^{n^{2}},*,T_{3}^{n^{2}+n},T_{2}^{n^{2}+n}).

Finally, once we prove the
T_{3}
-seminorm control of the third term in (
83
) using Proposition
7.4
, an iterated version of Proposition
7.5
, we derive seminorm control of other terms in (
83
) as follows. Replacing the third term in (
83
) by a dual function using the newly established
T_{3}
-control, Proposition
2.3
, and the pigeonhole principle, we get a tuple

\displaystyle(T_{1}^{n^{2}},T_{2}^{n^{2}},*,T_{2}^{n^{2}+n}),

and then we apply Proposition
6.6
to flip the
T_{2}
in the last term into
T_{4}
, obtaining the tuple

\displaystyle(T_{1}^{n^{2}},T_{2}^{n^{2}},*,T_{4}^{3n^{2}+3n}).

This tuple has length 3, and it is good for seminorm control by Proposition
7.1
applied inductively to tuples of length 3. Going back, this gives us seminorm control of the other terms in (
83
).

7.2.
Proof of Proposition
7.5

We prove Proposition
7.5
for the tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
of non-basic type
w
with the last nonzero index
t
, which is
a proper descendant of
\mathopen{}\left(T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
, by assuming that Proposition
7.1
holds for tuples of length
\ell-1
as well as length
\ell
and type
w^{\prime}<w
. For simplicity of notation, we assume that
m=\ell
satisfies the controllability condition.

By Proposition
3.7
, the vector
{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}
is nonzero and takes the form
{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}=b_{\ell}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}-b_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{i}}
for some
i\in\{0,\ldots,\ell-1\}
, where
b_{\ell},b_{i}
are the coefficients of
p_{\ell},p_{i}
of degree
d_{\ell i}:=\deg(p_{\ell}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}-p_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{i}})
. If
i=0
, then
b_{\ell}\neq 0
since
{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}
is nonzero. If
\eta_{i}=\eta_{\ell}
, then the controllability condition implies that
\rho_{i},\rho_{\ell}
are independent, and so
T^{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}
is a nonzero iterate of
T_{\eta_{\ell}}
. In both these cases, the result follows from the bound

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},c{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}}\ll_{|c|}\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}}

for any
c\neq 0
, which is a consequence of Lemma
2.1
.

For the case
\eta_{i}\neq\eta_{\ell}
and
\eta_{i}\in{\mathfrak{I}}_{t}
, recall first that
\eta_{\ell}\in{\mathfrak{I}}_{t}
by assumption, and the good ergodicity property of
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
implies that
\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{\ell}
have the good ergodicity property along
\eta
. Hence, we have
b_{\ell}=\beta_{\ell}\gcd(b_{\ell},b_{i})
,
b_{i}=\beta_{i}\gcd(b_{\ell},b_{i})
for coprime integers
\beta_{\ell},\beta_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
{\mathcal{I}}(T^{\beta_{\ell}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}-\beta_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{i}}})\subseteq{\mathcal{I}}(T_{\ell})
. By Lemmas
2.1
and
2.2
, we have

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}}\ll\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{\textbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\textbf{b}}_{s},\beta_{\ell}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}-\beta_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{i}}}\leq\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}}.

The last remaining case to consider, and the most difficult one, is when
\eta_{i}\in{\mathfrak{I}}_{t^{\prime}}
with
t^{\prime}\neq t
and
b_{\ell},b_{i}\neq 0
. The proof of Proposition
7.5
in this case follows the same two-step strategy that was explained in Example
1
, but we also have to take into account additional complications explained in Examples

7
and
8
. We first obtain the control of (
35
) by
\lvert\!|\!|f_{i}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{i}}^{\times s_{1}}}
for some
s_{1}\in{\mathbb{N}}
depending only on
d,D,\ell,L,s
. This is accomplished by using the control by
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}}
, given by assumption, for an appropriately defined function
\tilde{f_{\ell}}
in place of
f_{\ell}
. Subsequently, we repeat the procedure by applying the newly established control by
\lvert\!|\!|f_{i}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{i}}^{\times s_{1}}}
for a function
\tilde{f}_{i}
in place of
f_{i}
. This gives us the claimed result.

Step 1 (ping): Obtaining control by a seminorm of
f_{i}
.

Suppose that

(85)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{{\eta_{j}}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}f_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0.

The good invariance property of
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}
implies that
f_{\ell}
is invariant under
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{\ell}}^{a_{\eta_{\ell}}}T_{\ell}^{-a_{\ell}}\mathclose{}\right)^{\gamma}=T^{{\mathbf{c}}}
for some nonzero
\gamma>0
and
{\mathbf{c}}:=\gamma(a_{\eta_{\ell}}{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}-a_{\ell}{\mathbf{e}}_{\ell})
, where
a_{\eta_{\ell}},a_{\ell}
are the leading coefficients of
p_{\eta_{\ell}}
and
p_{\ell}
.
Combining this with Lemma
3.2
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell-1]}T_{{\eta_{j}}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}f_{j}\cdot T_{\eta_{\ell}}^{\rho_{\ell}(n)}\E(\tilde{f}_{\ell}|{\mathcal{I}}(T^{\mathbf{c}}))\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0

for some function

\tilde{f}_{\ell}:=\lim_{k\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N_{k}]}\,T_{\eta_{\ell}}^{-\rho_{\ell}(n)}g_{k}\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[\ell-1]\end{subarray}}T_{\eta_{\ell}}^{-\rho_{\ell}(n)}T_{{\eta_{j}}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}\overline{f}_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}T_{\eta_{\ell}}^{-\rho_{\ell}(n)}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n)),

where the limit is a weak limit. Then our assumption gives

\lvert\!|\!|\E(\tilde{f}_{\ell}|{\mathcal{I}}(T^{\mathbf{c}}))|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}}>0.

By Proposition
3.3
, we get

\liminf_{H\to\infty}\E_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s}}\\
\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell-1]}T_{{\eta_{j}}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{j})\cdot T_{\eta_{\ell}}^{\rho_{\ell}(n)}u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0,

where
u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}
are 1-bounded and invariant under both
T^{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}
and
T^{\mathbf{c}}
, and

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}(n):=\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(n)

is a product of
2^{s}
elements of
{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}
.
As a consequence of the
T^{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}
-invariance of
u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}
, we have

(86)

T_{\eta_{\ell}}^{b_{\ell}}u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}=T_{\eta_{i}}^{b_{i}}u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}},

where we use the identity
T_{\eta_{\ell}}^{b_{\ell}}=T_{\eta_{i}}^{b_{i}}T^{{\textbf{b}}_{s+1}}
. Let
\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}
be the smallest natural number such that
b_{\ell}
divides the coefficients of
\lambda\rho_{\ell}
.
By the triangle inequality,

\liminf_{H\to\infty}\E_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s}}\E_{r\in\{0,\ldots,\lambda-1\}}\\
\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell-1]}T_{{\eta_{j}}}^{\rho_{j}(\lambda n+r)}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{j})\cdot T_{\eta_{\ell}}^{\rho_{\ell}(\lambda n+r)}u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}(q_{j}(\lambda n+r))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0.

Using (
86
) and the pigeonhole principle, and setting
\eta^{\prime}=\tau_{\ell i}\eta
, we get that for some
r_{0}\in\{0,\ldots,\lambda-1\}
we have

(87)

\limsup_{H\to\infty}\E_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s}}\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{{\eta^{\prime}_{j}}}^{\rho^{\prime}_{j}(n)}f_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}(q^{\prime}_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0,

where

\displaystyle\rho^{\prime}_{j}(n)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\rho_{j}(\lambda n+r_{0})-\rho_{j}(r_{0}),\;&j\in[\ell-1]\\
\frac{b_{i}}{b_{\ell}}(\rho_{\ell}(\lambda n+r_{0})-\rho_{\ell}(r_{0})),&j=\ell\end{cases},

\displaystyle f_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(r_{0})}f_{j},\;&j\in[\ell-1]\\
T_{\eta_{\ell}}^{\rho_{\ell}(r_{0})}u_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}},\;&j=\ell\end{cases},

and
q^{\prime}_{j}(n):=q_{j}(\lambda n+r_{0})
. We note that the polynomials
\rho^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\rho^{\prime}_{\ell}
are as in Proposition
6.1
.

It follows from (
87
) that there exists a set
B\subset{\mathbb{N}}^{2s}
of positive upper density and
\varepsilon>0
such that

(88)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{{\eta^{\prime}_{j}}}^{\rho^{\prime}_{j}(n)}f_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}(q^{\prime}_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\geq\varepsilon

for every
({\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime})\in B
.

By assumption,
t
is the last nonzero index of
w
, implying that
t^{\prime}<t
. Hence, the new tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta^{\prime}_{j}}^{\rho^{\prime}_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
has type
w^{\prime}=\sigma_{tt^{\prime}}w<w
. Furthermore, by Proposition
6.5
, the functions
f_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}
have the good invariance property along
\eta^{\prime}
with respect to
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
. We therefore inductively apply Proposition
7.3
for tuples of length
\ell
and type
w^{\prime}
to each average (
88
). This allows us to conclude that there exist
s_{1}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, depending only on
d,D,\ell,L,s
, and
\delta>0
such that

\lvert\!|\!|\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{i}|\!|\!\rvert_{s_{1},T_{\eta_{i}}}\geq\delta

for
({\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime})\in B
.
Hence,

(89)

\displaystyle\limsup_{H\to\infty}\E_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s}}\lvert\!|\!|\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{i}|\!|\!\rvert_{s_{1},T_{\eta_{i}}}>0.

Together with Lemma
3.1
, the inductive formula for seminorms (
15
) and Hölder inequality, the inequality (
89
) implies that

\lvert\!|\!|f_{i}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{i}}^{\times s_{1}}}>0,

and so the seminorm
\lvert\!|\!|f_{i}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{i}}^{\times s_{1}}}
controls the average (
35
).

Step 2 (pong): Obtaining control by a seminorm of
f_{\ell}
.

To get the claim that
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}^{\times s^{\prime}}}
controls the average for some
s^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
depending only on
d,D,\ell,L,s
, we repeat the procedure once more with
f_{i}
in place of
f_{\ell}
. From (
85
) it follows that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[\ell],\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}T_{{\eta_{j}}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}f_{j}\cdot T_{\eta_{i}}^{\rho_{i}(n)}\tilde{f_{i}}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0

for some function

\tilde{f}_{i}:=\lim_{k\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N_{k}]}\,T_{\eta_{i}}^{-\rho_{i}(n)}g_{k}\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[\ell],\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}T_{\eta_{i}}^{-\rho_{i}(n)}T_{{\eta_{j}}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}\overline{f}_{j}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}T_{\eta_{i}}^{-\rho_{i}(n)}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n)),

where the limit is a weak limit. Then the previous result gives

\lvert\!|\!|\tilde{f}_{i}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{i}}^{\times s_{1}}}>0.

By Proposition
3.3
, we get

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}\E_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s}}\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[\ell],\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}T_{{\eta_{j}}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{j})\cdot\prod_{j\in[L+1]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0

where

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}(n):=\begin{cases}\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(n),\;&j\in[L]\\
T_{\eta_{i}}^{n}T^{-({\textbf{b}}_{1}h_{1}^{\prime}+\cdots+{\textbf{b}}_{s}h^{\prime}_{s})}\prod\limits_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}{\mathcal{D}}_{s_{1},T_{\eta_{i}}}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}^{{\underline{\epsilon}}}}\tilde{f}_{i}),\;&j=L+1.\end{cases}

Thus, the sequence of functions
{\mathcal{D}}_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}
is a product of
2^{s}
elements of
{\mathfrak{D}}_{d}
if
j\in[L]
, and it is a product of
2^{s}
elements of
{\mathfrak{D}}_{s_{1}}
for
j=L+1
. Consequently, there exists
\varepsilon>0
and a set
B^{\prime}\subset{\mathbb{N}}^{2s}
of positive lower density such that for every
({\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime})\in B^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[\ell],\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}T_{{\eta_{j}}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}(\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{j})\cdot\prod_{j\in[L+1]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>\varepsilon.

Proposition
6.5
implies that the functions
(g_{1,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}},\ldots,g_{\ell,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}})_{({\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2s}}
given by

\displaystyle g_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}:=\begin{cases}\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{j},\;&j\neq i\\
1,\;&j=i\end{cases}

have the good invariance property along
\eta
with respect to
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
. Proposition
6.6
then gives polynomials
\rho_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\rho_{\ell}^{\prime},q^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,q^{\prime}_{L}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
, 1-bounded functions
g^{\prime}_{1,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}},\ldots,g^{\prime}_{\ell,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}
with
g^{\prime}_{i,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}:=1
such that

(90)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[\ell],\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}T_{j}^{\rho^{\prime}_{j}(n)}g^{\prime}_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}\cdot\prod_{j\in[L+1]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}(q^{\prime}_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>\varepsilon.

It also gives a constant
C>0
depending only on
\gamma
and the leading coefficients of
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
such that

(91)

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|g^{\prime}_{j,{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}|\!|\!\rvert_{s^{\prime},T_{j}}\leq C\lvert\!|\!|\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s^{\prime},T_{\eta_{j}}}

for all
j\in[\ell]\setminus\{i\}
,
({\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime})\in B^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime}\geq 2
.
Observing that the averages (
90
) have length
\ell-1
and
\rho^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\rho^{\prime}_{i-1},\rho^{\prime}_{i+1},\ldots,\rho^{\prime}_{\ell}
have the good ergodicity property for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{i-1},T_{i+1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
(another consequence of Propositions
6.6
and
6.4
), we conclude from (
91
) and Proposition
7.3
that there exist
s^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
(depending only on
d,D,\ell,L,s
) and
\delta>0
satisfying

\displaystyle\lvert\!|\!|\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{s^{\prime},T_{\eta_{\ell}}}>\delta

for every
({\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime})\in B^{\prime}
. Consequently, we deduce that

\displaystyle\liminf_{H\to\infty}\E_{{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in[H]^{s}}\lvert\!|\!|\Delta_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}};{\underline{h}}-{\underline{h}}^{\prime}}f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{s^{\prime},T_{\eta_{\ell}}}>0.

It then follows from Lemma
3.1
and the Hölder inequality that
\lvert\!|\!|f_{\ell}|\!|\!\rvert_{{{\textbf{b}}_{1}},\ldots,{{\textbf{b}}_{s}},{\mathbf{e}}_{\eta_{\ell}}^{\times s^{\prime}}}>0
, as claimed.

7.3.
Proof of Proposition
7.1

We induct on the length
\ell
of the average, and for each fixed
\ell
we further induct on type.
In the base case
\ell=1
, Proposition
7.1
follows directly from Proposition
3.7
. We assume therefore that the average has length
\ell>1
and type
w
and the statement holds for averages of length
\ell-1
as well as length
\ell
and type
w^{\prime}<w
. If the type
w
is basic, then Proposition
7.1
follows from Proposition
7.2
, so we assume that
w
is not basic. We argue differently depending on whether the average is controllable or not.

Case 1: controllable averages.

If the average is controllable, then Proposition
7.4
gives
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
depending only on
d,D,\ell,L
as well as
m\in[\ell]
such that (
80
) holds whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{m}}}=0
. Suppose now that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{{\eta_{j}}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}f_{j}\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0.

Applying the fact that
\lvert\!|\!|f_{m}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{m}}}
controls this average, Proposition
2.3
and the pigeonhole principle, we replace
f_{m}
by a dual function of level
s
, so that we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[\ell],\\
j\neq m\end{subarray}}T_{{\eta_{j}}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}f_{j}\cdot{\mathcal{D}}(\rho_{j}(n))\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0

for some
{\mathcal{D}}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{s}
. By Proposition
6.6
, there exist 1-bounded functions
(f_{j}^{\prime})_{j\in[\ell]}
with
f^{\prime}_{m}=1
and polynomials
\rho_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\rho_{\ell}^{\prime},q_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,q_{L}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[\ell],\\
j\neq m\end{subarray}}T_{j}^{\rho_{j}^{\prime}(n)}f_{j}^{\prime}\cdot{\mathcal{D}}(\rho_{j}(n))\cdot\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}^{\prime}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0.

Provided
s\geq 2
(which we can assume without loss of generality), Proposition
6.6
also implies that
\lvert\!|\!|f^{\prime}_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{j}}\leq C\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{j}}}
for a constant
C>0
depending only on the leading coefficients of
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
and the number
\gamma
for which
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}
have the
\gamma
-invariance property along
\eta
. Moreover, the fact that
\rho^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\rho^{\prime}_{\ell}
are descendants of
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
and Proposition
6.4
imply that
\mathopen{}\left(T_{j}^{\rho_{j}^{\prime}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell],j\neq m}
has the good ergodicity property. By the case
\ell-1
of Proposition
7.1
, we deduce that
\lvert\!|\!|f^{\prime}_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{j}}>0
for
j\neq m
, and hence
\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{j}}}>0
for
j\neq m
.

Case 2: uncontrollable averages.

If the average is uncontrollable, then we apply Proposition
6.8
to deduce the existence of polynomials
q_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,q_{L}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[n]
, a tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{j}^{\prime}}^{\rho^{\prime}_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
of type
w^{\prime}<w
that is a proper descendant of
\mathopen{}\left(T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
as well as functions
f_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,f_{\ell}^{\prime}\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
satisfying

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{{\eta_{j}}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}f_{j}\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\E_{n\in[N]}\,\prod_{j\in[\ell]}T_{{\eta^{\prime}_{j}}}^{\rho^{\prime}_{j}(n)}f^{\prime}_{j}\prod_{j\in[L]}{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(q^{\prime}_{j}(n))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

and
\lvert\!|\!|f^{\prime}_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta^{\prime}_{j}}}\leq C\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{j}}}
for every
s\geq 2
and
j\in[\ell]
as well as some constant
C>0
depending only on
\gamma
and the leading coefficients of
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
. Moreover, the functions
f^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,f^{\prime}_{\ell}
have the good invariance property along
\eta^{\prime}
with respect to
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
. By the induction hypothesis, there exists
s\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that the second average above vanishes whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f^{\prime}_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta^{\prime}_{j}}}=0
, and so the first average also vanishes whenever
\lvert\!|\!|f_{j}|\!|\!\rvert_{s,T_{\eta_{j}}}=0
. This establishes the seminorm control over the tuple
\mathopen{}\left(T_{\eta_{j}}^{\rho_{j}(n)}\mathclose{}\right)_{j\in[\ell]}
.

8.
Proofs of joint ergodicity results

In this section, we derive Theorem
1.2
and Corollaries
1.3
and
1.4
. We start with two observations that connect the notions of joint ergodicity and weak joint ergodicity. Their proofs are straightforward, hence we skip them.

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T)
be a system. Suppose that there exists a sequence
a:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
(T^{a(n)})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is ergodic for
\mu
. Then
T
is ergodic.

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}
be sequences and
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
be a system. The sequences are jointly ergodic for the system if and only if they are weakly jointly ergodic and the transformations
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
are ergodic.

We continue with the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Suppose first that the polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
have the good ergodicity property for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
. By Theorem
1.1
, this implies that
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
are good for the seminorm control for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
. This result, property (ii), and Theorem
2.4
imply that
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
are weakly jointly ergodic for the system.

Conversely, suppose that the polynomials are weakly jointly ergodic for the system. The condition (ii) follows by taking
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}
to be nonergodic eigenfunctions of respective transformations. To prove condition (i), suppose that
p_{i}/c_{i}=p_{j}/c_{j}
for some
i\neq j
and coprime integers
c_{i},c_{j}
, and there exists a function
f
invariant under
T_{i}^{c_{i}}T_{j}^{-c_{j}}
that is not simultaneously invariant under
T_{i}
and
T_{j}
. The invariance property of
f
gives
T_{i}^{c_{i}}f=T_{j}^{c_{j}}{f}
, and the same holds for
\overline{f}
. The coprimeness of
c_{i},c_{j}
implies that the polynomial
p_{i}/c_{i}=p_{j}/c_{j}
has integer coefficients, and so we have

\displaystyle\E_{n\in[N]}T_{i}^{p_{i}(n)}f\cdot T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}\overline{f}=\E_{n\in[N]}T_{i}^{p_{i}(n)}|f|^{2}=\E_{n\in[N]}T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}|f|^{2},

which by the weak joint ergodicity of
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
converges to
\E(|f|^{2}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{i}))=\E(|f|^{2}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{j}))
in
L^{2}(\mu)
. On the other hand, the weak joint ergodicity of
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
implies that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}T_{i}^{p_{i}(n)}f\cdot T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)}\overline{f}=\E(f|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{i}))\cdot\overline{\E(f|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{j}))}

in
L^{2}(\mu)
. Hence,
\E(|f|^{2}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{i}))=\E(f|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{i}))\cdot\overline{\E(f|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{j}))}
. The properties of the conditional expectation and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality imply that

\displaystyle\int\mathopen{}\left|\E(f|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{i}))\cdot\overline{\E(f|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{j}))}\mathclose{}\right|\,d\mu

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\E(f|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{i}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\cdot\left\|\E(f|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{j}))\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}=\int|f|^{2}\,d\mu=\int\E(|f|^{2}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{i}))\,d\mu.

The two inequalities above become an equality precisely when
f=\E(f|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{i}))=\E(f|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{j}))
holds
\mu
-a.e., i.e. when
f
is simultaneously invariant under
T_{i}
and
T_{j}
, and so either this is the case, contradicting the assumptions on
f
, or
\E(|f|^{2}|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{i}))\neq\E(f|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{i}))\cdot\overline{\E(f|{\mathcal{I}}(T_{j}))}
, contradicting the weak joint ergodicity of
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
.
∎

We now derive Corollary
1.3
from Theorem
1.2
.

Proof of Corollary
1.3
.

By Lemma
8.2
, the polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
are jointly ergodic for the system
(X,{\mathcal{X}},\mu,T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell})
if and only if they are weakly jointly ergodic for this system and the transformations
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
are ergodic. Theorem
1.2
in turn implies that this is equivalent to the system having the good ergodicity property, the transformations
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
being ergodic, and the equation (
6
) holding for all eigenfunctions. Since the transformations are ergodic, all the eigenfunctions
\chi_{j}
of
T_{j}
satisfy
T_{j}\chi_{j}=\lambda_{j}\chi_{j}
for a constant
\lambda_{j}
, and so the condition (
6
) reduces to (
7
) upon taking
\lambda_{j}=e(\alpha_{j})
and realising that
\int\chi_{j}\,d\mu=0
unless
\alpha_{j}=0
. Lastly, the good ergodicity property and the ergodicity of the transformations
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
jointly imply the very good ergodicity property.
∎

Finally, we prove Corollary
1.4
.

Proof of Corollary
1.4
.

The forward direction follows from
[
7
, Proposition 5.3]
, and so it is enough to deduce the reverse direction. Our goal is to show that the conditions (i) and (ii) in the statement of Conjecture
1
imply the conditions (i) and (ii) in the statement of Corollary
1.3
. The condition (ii) in Conjecture
1
, i.e. the ergodicity of
(T_{1}^{p_{1}(n)},\ldots,T_{\ell}^{p_{\ell}(n)})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, implies the condition (ii) in Corollary
1.3
by taking eigenfunctions. By Lemma
8.1
, it also implies the ergodicity of
T_{1},\ldots,T_{\ell}
because each sequence
(T_{j}^{p_{j}(n)})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is ergodic.

To establish the very good ergodicity property of
p_{1},\ldots,p_{\ell}
, suppose that
p_{i}/c_{i}=p_{j}/c_{j}
for some relatively prime
c_{i},c_{j}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
f
is a nonconstant function invariant under
T_{i}^{c_{i}}T_{j}^{-c_{j}}
. Letting
q:=p_{i}/c_{i}=p_{j}/c_{j}
and noting that it has integer coefficients due to the coprimeness of
c_{i},c_{j}
, we observe that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}T_{i}^{p_{i}(n)}T_{j}^{-p_{j}(n)}f=\lim_{N\to\infty}\E_{n\in[N]}(T_{i}^{c_{i}}T_{j}^{-c_{j}})^{q(n)}f=f\neq\int f\,d\mu,

contradicting the ergodicity of
(T_{i}^{p_{i}(n)}T_{j}^{-p_{j}(n)})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
.
∎
